\u0000 \u0000\n<\n\u0000p\u0000>\u0000\" T\u0000h\u0000e\u0000 \u0000l\u0000i\u0000f\u0000t\u0000i\u0000n\u0000g\u0000 \u0000o\u0000f\u0000 \u0000t\u0000h\u0000e\u0000 \u0000c\u0000u\u0000r\u0000f\u0000e\u0000w\u0000 \u0000h\u0000a\u0000s\u0000 \u0000h\u0000a\u0000d\u0000 \u0000a\u0000 \u0000p\u0000o\u0000s\u0000i\u0000t\u0000i\u0000v\u0000e\u0000 \u0000i\u0000m\u0000p\u0000a\u0000c\u0000t\u0000 \u0000w\u0000i\u0000t\u0000h\u0000 \u0000r\u0000e\u0000s\u0000p\u0000e\u0000c\u0000t\u0000 \u0000t\u0000o\u0000 \u0000t\u0000h\u0000e\u0000 \u0000r\u0000i\u0000g\u0000h\u0000t\u0000 \u0000t\u0000o\u0000 \u0000f\u0000r\u0000e\u0000e\u0000d\u0000o\u0000m\u0000 \u0000o\u0000f\u0000 \u0000m\u0000o\u0000v\u0000e\u0000m\u0000e\u0000n\u0000t\u0000.\u0000\n<\n\u0000/\u0000p\u0000>\u0000 \u0000 \u0000 \u0000 \u0000 \u0000 \u0000 \u0000 \u0000\n<\n\u0000h\u00003\u0000>\u0000H\u0000.\u0000R\u0000e\u0000p\u0000l\u0000y\u0000 \u0000t\u0000o\u0000 \u0000t\u0000h\u0000e\u0000 \u0000q\u0000u\u0000e\u0000s\u0000t\u0000i\u0000o\u0000n\u0000 \u0000r\u0000a\u0000i\u0000s\u0000e\u0000d\u0000 \u0000i\u0000n\u0000 \u0000p\u0000a\u0000r\u0000a\u0000g\u0000r\u0000a\u0000p\u0000h\u0000 \u00004\u0000\n<\n\u0000/\u0000h\u00003\u0000>\u0000 \u0000 \u0000 \u0000 \u0000 \u0000 \u0000 \u0000 \u0000", "excerpt_sha256": "90552ede8399d8162ebf82b89b3761f23b8d7f2ca841b3020fb8b3141e1e24b6", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt does not support the claims about the technical team not being formalized or reliable statistics still being compiled."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0011-E016", "source_id": "SRC-6a30815c9b8067e6", "url": "https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/17502977.2025.2532979", "title": "‘We Don't Want Another Observer Mission’: The East African Force and the Politics of Deployment in the DRC", "publisher": "Taylor & Francis", "raw_sha256": "2558240d4ea74e3022312568621b412fb103ce2f5e1ed6848518e5abac68f974", "fact_summary": "In 2023, disagreements over the East African Community Regional Force's mandate intensified. President Tshisekedi sought regional partners to fight M23, but the force emphasized diplomacy and did not conduct the expected offensive operations; the Congolese government concluded that it had not achieved expected results and ended the mandate, with the last Kenyan troops departing on 21 December 2023.", "question_ids": ["2B.1", "2B.10", "2B.7", "2B.9", "8B.10", "8B.9"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The force's deployment, disagreement, and withdrawal occurred during 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The article directly attributes the search for regional partners and the decision to discontinue the EAC mandate to Tshisekedi's government.", "limitations": ["The article is based on research conducted in 2022-2023 and presents competing regional and Congolese interpretations.", "It does not establish that Tshisekedi's strategy caused the military stalemate alone."], "start_unit": 9, "end_unit": 10, "locator": "extracted HTML block 9 through extracted HTML block 10", "exact_excerpt": "use of force\nDemocratic Republic of Congo\nEast African community\nIntroduction\nOn the 21st of December 2023 the last Kenyan troops from the ‘East African Community Regional Force’ (EACRF) departed from the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) – roughly one year after their arrival in November 2022. After the United Nations (UN) had announced that its operation in the DRC (MONUSCO) may reach its limit in fighting the newly emerging offensive by the ‘Mouvement du 23-Mars’ (M23) in June 2022, the DRC’s President Tshisekedi started looking eastwards hoping to find reliable partners to fight M23 (Besheer\nCitation\n2022\n). Yet, instead of fostering peace in Congo’s eastern provinces, controversies over the EACRF’s positionality and strategy hindered any substantial progress. By 2023, escalating disagreements over its role – particularly regarding M23 – led EAC leaders to withdraw the mission, leaving behind a rather mixed record.\nBased on discussions about the ongoing militarisation of international and regional peacekeeping (Bellamy and Hunt\nCitation\n2021\n; Bode and Karlsrud\nCitation\n2019\n; Bourgeois and Labuda\nCitation\n2023\n; Curran and Hunt\nCitation\n2020\n; Day and Hunt\nCitation\n2022\n; Goulart\nCitation\n\n2023\n; Hunt\nCitation\n2017\n; Russo\nCitation\n2021\n) and the introduction to this Special Issue (Witt\nCitation\n2026\n), this article zooms into the EACRF as an example highlighting the challenges of contemporary regional peace enforcement operations. Through this, the following analysis will address a research gap, not only on the crucial role regional leaders have played in re-interpreting peace and security related norms, but also on process from below, which have contributed to an increased level of politicisation of the efforts by civil society and activists from the host country. While academic attention has largely focused on elite processes at the UN and regional levels, the EACRF case demonstrates that when regional organisations deploy, normative contestation across interconnected levels has become the norm rather than the exception. I argue that mandate interpretation and implementation has become highly politicised. My analysis highlights how conflicting regional, national, and local interests and expectations – particularly concerning the EACRF’s overarching strategy and the use of military force – have posed insurmountable challenges to the operation, paradoxically intensifying rather than alleviating certain local, national as well as regional tensions.\nBased on research conducted in 2022 and 2023, this article centres on how the Congolese government and Congolese non-state actors perceived the EARCF’s presence in the DRC.\nFootnote\n1\nImmersing myself in EACRF discourse, I found that regional ambivalence over peace enforcement mandates created a context-specific ‘battlefield of knowledge’ (Lie\nCitation\n2012\n). Given the absence of a universally accepted interpretation of norms and principles guiding regional peace efforts, normative contestation is inevitable. These normative tensions, in turn, may render such efforts susceptible to power struggles and competing interests among regional actors. Thus, while regional efforts undoubtedly constitute an increasingly important expression of African agency in peace and security (see Yaw Tchie\nCitation\n2023\n, 427), I contend their deployment in complex contexts – where actors tend to engage based on their own vested political, economic and security interests – may in fact run the risk of undermining regional peace, especially in a ‘rough neighbourhood’ (Khadiagala\nCitation\n2022\n, 43) like the Great Lakes Region. Accordingly, this article engages with the overarching themes of this special issue as outlined in the introduction (Witt\nCitation", "excerpt_sha256": "762e5f84789febc976b78315c9b3e39d3d6ec3eed57214a217bd8824756813a0", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0011-E017", "source_id": "SRC-6a30815c9b8067e6", "url": "https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/17502977.2025.2532979", "title": "‘We Don't Want Another Observer Mission’: The East African Force and the Politics of Deployment in the DRC", "publisher": "Taylor & Francis", "raw_sha256": "2558240d4ea74e3022312568621b412fb103ce2f5e1ed6848518e5abac68f974", "fact_summary": "The EACRF's political track excluded M23 because Kinshasa rejected political dialogue with the group; the force's military deadlines passed without full M23 withdrawal, and neither the EAC military deployment nor diplomatic efforts produced measurable security improvement according to the article's cited assessment. Tshisekedi adopted a hardline stance, accused regional contingents of cohabiting with M23 and collecting taxes, and publicly escalated tensions with Rwanda during the election period.", "question_ids": ["2B.1", "2B.10", "2B.2", "2B.3", "2B.7", "8B.1", "8B.9"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The described policy positions, accusations, and security outcomes concern 2023, with some background from late 2022.", "ruler_attribution": "The article attributes rejection of dialogue and the hardline stance to Tshisekedi's government and reports his public accusations.", "limitations": ["Some claims are reported interpretations or attributed quotations rather than independently verified findings.", "The article notes regional interests and M23/Rwanda involvement as important external factors."], "start_unit": 19, "end_unit": 25, "locator": "extracted HTML block 19 through extracted HTML block 25", "exact_excerpt": "Citation\nn.d.\n)\nThat being said, the EAC stressed that the security situation in the DRC would require a political solution. The political track would facilitate high-level negotiations with different armed groups in the context of the Nairobi and Luanda Process. Yet, these talks did not include the M23, as Kinshasa rejected any political dialogue with them (Maliro\nCitation\n2023\n). Despite this challenge, the bloc hoped to facilitate the ‘implementation of the political and military tracks’, which would be complementing each other accordingly (East African Community\nCitation\n2022b\n).\nPerhaps most importantly, the EAC clarified that if violent groups like M23 would not adhere, they would enforce compliance militarily. In a meeting between Tshisekedi and Kenyatta of November 2022 both leaders re-confirmed ‘that foreign armed groups that do not voluntarily surrender and return to their countries of origin are forcefully ejected from the territory of the DRC’ (East African Community\nCitation\n2022a\n). Similarly, the Luanda-Summit at the end of November 2022 stressed that the EACRF was ready to intervene against M23 ‘in case of non-compliance by M23 to cease hostilities and withdraw from occupied territories’ (East African Community\nCitation\n2022d\n). Citing an official document by the Chiefs of General Staff of the EACRF, the summit re-confirmed that ‘if M23 does not withdraw the EAC Heads of States shall authorise the use of force to compel the group to comply’ (East African Community\nCitation\n2022c\n).\nThese statements demonstrate an assumed willingness to implement a military heavy interpretation of EACRF’s mandate on the ground. Yet, beyond the discursive level, this military avenue never fully transpired. Official deadlines passed, with the M23 only withdrawing partially from certain regions, recapturing other territories at the same time.\nFootnote\n10\nShortly after the summit in Luanda, the M23 announced that any ceasefire agreement between African heads of states would not concern them, as they were not included in any negotiations (Aljazeera\nCitation\n\n2022b\n). Only in late December 2022, did the M23 started pulling out of certain towns like Kibumba as a ‘goodwill gesture’ (Radio Okapi\nCitation\n2022\n). Against the backdrop of the continued presence of the M23 in Eastern DRC and the unwillingness of the EACRF to engage in combat to enforce compliance, the government in Kinshasa ultimately decided to discontinue the EAC’s mandate. Claiming that ‘the force has not achieved the expected results within the agreed deadlines,’ Patrick Muyaya, spokesperson of the Congolese government, announced the EAC’s departure for December 2023 (Actualité\nCitation\n2023\n).\nIn contrast, the EAC stressed the crucial role it played in protecting Goma and facilitating the withdrawal of the M23 along important logistical roads to Goma. The Kenyan Chief of Defence Forces General Francis Ogolla highlighted his satisfaction with the Kenyan troops, as they played their ‘role professionally with minimal collateral’ (East African Community\nCitation\n2023\n). In the following section I will analyse some of the challenges, namely the differing expectations and perceptions by regional leaders, the Congolese government and parts of Congolese civil society, around the use of military force. As my analysis will show, the actual interpretation and factual implementation of the EACRF’s mandate have remained a point of regional contestation throughout, with different regional actors competing over its normative meaning.\n‘We are not here to fight’ – The regional approach\nThe EACRF’s development and its ultimate departure from the DRC needs to be analysed against the backdrop of the bloc’s positionality in the conflict, the changing rhetoric of its prominent leaders and ultimate unwillingness to apply the use of force on the ground. From the beginning on, Rwandan President Paul Kagame (in The East African\nCitation\n2023a\n) stressed that the issue of M23 required a political approach, addressing the root causes of the underlying problems to avoid providing ‘a partial solution’. Contrary to Kenyatta’s early announcements, regional leaders substantially re-interpreted the EACRF’s supposed role in the DRC along these lines. Instead, they pushed for a strengthening of the Nairobi Process, which had started in November 2022, to demobilise armed groups (see Chome\nCitation\n2024\n).\nMoreover, some regional leaders started framing the EACRF as a non-offensive mission. In this moment, it became clear that the actual implementation of the EACRF’s mandate was, in Lie’s words, ‘open for interpretation and contestation by those acting in its name’ (\nCitation\n2012\n, 144). Stressing diplomacy, regional leaders reinterpreted the EACRF’s mandate to support the political process and monitor the ceasefire’s implementation. The following statement by Uganda’s President Museveni is illustrative in this regard:\nWe are going to be in the Bunagana-Rutshuru area, not in order to fight the M23, but to act as a neutral force as the Congolese use the time to sort out their political problems. Unless we are attacked, we are not there to fight’. (in Olukya\n\nCitation\n2023\n)\nSimilarily, the Ugandan Lieutenant General Kayanja Muhanga called attention to fundamental differences between the EACRF and the Operation Shujaa, which the UPDF had launched against ADF in November 2021. Unlike the latter, Muhanga stressed, the EACRF was not there to engage in combat but ‘to witness a process of achieving peace in Rutshuru territory and the neighbouring areas’ (The East African\nCitation\n2023c\n). As a result, the communiqué launching the third inter-Congolese dialogue under the Nairobi Process, confirmed the ‘emphasis on the Political Process [sic!] as the surest path to sustainable peace and security’ (East African Community\nCitation\n2022e\n) in November 2022.\nAlthough Kenyatta initially spoke of ‘enforcing peace,’ aligning with stabilisation and peace enforcement frameworks, Tanzania’s President Samia Suluhu Hassan later suggested that regional leaders see their commitment to peace in Eastern DRC as more closely aligned with traditional peacekeeping norms:\nEast Africa has also sent their soldiers there. They are there and their work is just to do peacekeeping. We are not going to fight, we are going to talk, to do peacekeeping, to speak with these terrorists or rebels, which threaten peace so that they agree with the regional demands.\nFootnote\n11\nThis normative re-interpretation from a robust to a rather traditional peacekeeping approach demonstrate the normative ambivalence regarding the EACRF’s mandate. It shows how regional leaders, as ‘mediators between policies and practice’ (Lie\nCitation\n2012\n, 145), used the operation’s normative openness to project their own interpretations of the mandate and competing visions of peace, shaped by their interests and regional positions in the DRC.\nThese normative and rhetoric shifts also translated into practice on the ground. Apart from Burundian troops, who often denied their involvement in offensive operations against the M23, the actual use of force had remained rather limited.\nFootnote\n12\nYet, this re-interpretation did neither sit well with Congolese elites nor with non-state actors. As Williams (\nCitation\n2017\n, 128) states: ‘Geographic proximity to a conflict does not automatically generate a regional consensus on how to respond’. The same applies to the DRC, where the EACRF’s mandate became highly politicised. Actors across different levels engaged in ‘knowledge battles’ (Lie\n\nCitation\n2012\n, 145) over its interpretation and implementation. While the EAC ultimately favoured political over military means and pushed for the Nairobi Process, the government in Kinshasa and many Congolese non-state actors contested this interpretation. They largely rejected any form of political dialogue and accused Kenya of siding with Rwanda (Chome\nCitation\n2024\n). As the following will illuminate, the EAC’s reluctancy to interpret the mandate militarily, led to tensions and even outright conflict between the Congolese government and the EAC’s member states.\n‘Co-Habitation was not part of the plan’ – Critique by the Congolese government\nWhen military victories failed to materialise and the M23’s compliance was not enforced militarily, it became clear that there was no shared understanding of the EACRF’s role among regional leaders, but that the mandate was in fact ‘open for interpretation’ (Lie\nCitation\n2012\n, 145). After protests in Goma against the EACRF in February 2023, the Congolese Deputy Prime Minister Christophe Lutundula reminded all stakeholders, ‘that the mandate of the Regional Force is unequivocally offensive’ (in Ilunga\nCitation\n2023a\n). Over the coming weeks Kinshasa continued to assert that the EACRF was not adhering to the agreed SOFA. If this continued, Lutundua warned that Kinshasa was ready to ask for the EACRF’s departure (The East African\nCitation\n2023b\n). Scaling up the rhetoric even further, Christophe Mboso, speaker of the Congolese National Assembly, stressed in March 2023 that the EACRF had been executing its offensive mandate too slowly, hereby demonstrating a lack of support for DRC. For him, the EACRF in fact offered nothing but ‘a picture of troops on holiday in the Congolese theatre of operations’ (in Ilunga\nCitation\n2023b\n).\nWhile these statements highlight the Congolese government’s normative interpretation of the EACRF’s mandate as, first and foremost offensive, the force has also faced accusations of outright collaboration with violent groups. Tshisekedi scaled up his rhetoric in May 2023:\nwe have noticed certain contingents among this force, except Burundi, who is putting all the efforts in the mission as it was defined; other contingents are now living with M23, collecting taxes in the zone that they are illegally occupying. That's a genuine problem, and it also compels us to question the purpose of the EAC mission. (in The East African\nCitation\n2023c\n)\n\nIn another statement, Tshisekedi stressed that he has noticed a certain ‘co-habitation’ between ‘the regional force of Eastern Africa and the terrorists of M23’, once again questioning the role the EAC was playing in the conflict (in Dube\nCitation\n2023\n).\nThese accusations must be understood both in the historical context of the Great Lakes Region and in light of the DRC’s internal political dynamics. As briefly stated above, certain regional leaders had historically supported different violent groups operating in the DRC, in order to safeguard their own national security and economic interests since the First and Second Congo War. As van de Walle (\nCitation\n2022\n) notes, ‘several of the DRC’s neighbours have repeatedly and deliberately undermined stability in its east by bolstering proxy fighters and tapping its huge natural resources’. Since the 1990s, there has been a constant competition over access and control over the DRC’s rich mineral resources, such as coltan and cobalt. Oftentimes this regional competition is tied to the collaboration or active support of armed groups as proxies. While the link between natural resources and conflict is complex (Vogel & Raeymaekers, 2016), neighbouring countries’ interests are significant. Many Congolese regard them as exploitation of the DRC’s wealth and a key driver of war (see IPIS, ASSODIP, and DIIS\nCitation\n2024\n; see also Birchinger, Jaw, and Witt\nCitation\n2026\nin this Special Issue).\nRwanda’s exclusion from contributing troops to the EACRF stemmed from its strained relations with the DRC, rooted in its involvement in the First and Second Congo Wars and the ongoing instability in Eastern DRC. Tensions between Rwanda and the DRC continued to rise during EACRF’s deployment, as an increasing amount of evidence suggested that Rwanda actively supports the M23 (UN Group of Experts\nCitation\n2023\n, 9–10). Accordingly, it might not come as a surprise that Tshisekedi changed his strategy from fostering stronger regional ties at the beginning of his first term towards outright confrontation (see Khadiagala\nCitation\n2022\n, 50). Particularly during run up to the December 2023 elections, he was unwilling to risk losing domestic support by engaging in dialogue with the M23, widely regarded by many Congolese as the nation's primary enemy (Congo Research Group\nCitation\n2023\n). This domestic dimension fed significantly into regional tensions. With elections approaching and President Tshisekedi in a politically weakened position, the need to demonstrate tangible results in the East became pressing. Also, it became opportune to shift blame for the persisting insecurities in the East to regional leaders and hereby deflect accountability. Accordingly, he adopted ‘a hardline stance towards the EACRF’ and intensified cooperation with local armed groups to confront the M23 (Bauma\nCitation\n\n2024\n, 9). During this time, ‘anyone advocating for negotiations was branded a traitor’ (Titeca\nCitation\n2025\n). In line with this position, President Tshisekedi escalated tensions with Kigali by publicly declaring that he would initiate a war with Rwanda if re-elected. Though this statement was made in the context of electoral campaigning, it nevertheless reflects Kinshasa’s radical stance and the escalating tensions between Rwanda and the DRC, both members of the EAC (see Musoke\nCitation\n2024\n; see also Khadiagala\nCitation\n2022\n, 52).\nAdding to these tensions with Rwanda, there is also evidence that Ugandan troops were standing by when the M23 took over Bunagana in June 2022 (UN Group of Experts\nCitation\n2022\n, 12). Stearns claims that Uganda, while fighting ADF in North Kivu, also started actively supporting the M23 in its recent fight (in Bashi\nCitation\n2022\n).\nFootnote\n13\nThough some scholars argue that Uganda’s support is much more passive than Rwanda’s, it is clear that ‘[l]eaving eastern DRC or M23 only under the influence of Rwanda is seen as a threat’ (Titeca\nCitation\n2024\n; see also Miviri\nCitation\n\n2024\n) to Kampala’s interests in the DRC.\nThese examples illustrate how regional rivalries, conflicting interests and shifting alliances have substantially shaped relations in the Great Lakes regions. Against this backdrop, it is perhaps unsurprising that the EACRF demonstrated only limited willingness – or capacity – to implement Kenyatta’s initial vision of a robust force or to fulfil Tshisekedi’s expectation of active combat engagement. While Rwanda has not been a troop contributor, Verweijen and Vogel (\nCitation\n2023\n) claim that regional leaders did shy away from a military combat because they did not want ‘to antagonize Rwanda’. Instead, they began advocating for dialogue through the Nairobi Process. As Kenya’s incumbent President Ruto stated, this approach aimed to foster a ‘greater understanding and coming together’ within the region (The East African\nCitation\n2023a\n).\nYet, this level of cooperation remained an illusion – especially given that Tshisekedi’s government has largely continued to refuse the EAC’s diplomacy-oriented approach. In this sense, one might argue that political contestation in the ‘battlefield of knowledge’ (Lie\nCitation\n2012\n) over the EACRF’s mandate turned into outright political conflict at the regional level.\nOn the ground, ‘neither the EAC military deployment nor the diplomatic efforts produced any measurable improvement in the security situation’ (Khadiagala\nCitation\n2022\n, 52). In fact, the lack of a shared normative understanding of the EACRF’s mandate and constant contestation over its role in Eastern DRC continued to deteriorate regional relations. The relationship between Kinshasa and Nairobi, in particular, reached a low point when President Tshisekedi decided to withdraw his government's ambassador from Kenya. This decision followed the formation of a new umbrella organisation called the ‘Alliance Fleuve Congo’ (AFC), which prominently included members of M23’s leadership and openly aimed to overthrow the Congolese government, capturing first Goma and then Bukavu in 2025 (Huon\nCitation\n2025\n).\nWhile the Kenyan government stated that it was proud of the work it had done in the DRC and stressed that the EACRF had in fact ‘made tremendous progress’ (East African Community Twitter Profile\nCitation\n2023\n), perceptions in Kinshasa seems to be the exact opposite. However, it was not only the relationships between regional leaders that worsened. The following section examines the criticisms of the EACRF voiced by Congolese civil society, including groups such as LUCHA, and explores how the mandate has been politicised at this level.\n‘We through extracted HTML block 4", "exact_excerpt": "The Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) authorities must immediately lift the state of siege, which is similar to a state of emergency, that has been in force in the provinces of Nord-Kivu and Ituri for two years, as it violates the country’s Constitution and international human rights law, Amnesty International said today.\nThe state of siege is unlawful and has contributed to the worsening of the human rights situation in the country.\nTigere Chagutah, East and Southern Africa Regional Director, Amnesty International\n“The state of siege is unlawful and has contributed to the worsening of the human rights situation in the country. President Felix Tshisekedi must lift this repressive measure without delay,” said Tigere Chagutah, Amnesty International’s Regional Director for East and Southern Africa.\n“Both the enforcement and repeated extensions of the rights-restricting ‘state of siege’ fall far short of meeting the DRC’s own constitution and international human rights obligations.”\nThe stated objective of the state of siege was to “rapidly improve civilian protection, curb armed groups, and restore State authority”, yet the security situation in the two provinces has drastically deteriorated. An upsurge of attacks against civilians and civilian casualties has been\nreported\nin the region over the last two years.\nPresident Felix Tshisekedi must lift this repressive measure without delay.\nTigere Chagutah\nNonetheless, the state of siege has been extended over 50 times by the government, without any meaningful public debate on the legality or merit of these extensions.\n“If President Tshisekedi fails to lift the state of siege now, after two years of unlawful and unjustified restrictions of human rights, members of parliament must reject any new requests from the government to extend it,” said Tigere Chagutah.\nDespite the state of siege, the DRC army has persistently failed to prevent or respond to increasing attacks against civilians by armed groups. According to information gathered by the\nUN Joint Human Rights Office\n, the army has in fact been responsible for committing numerous serious human rights violations against civilians.\nAmnesty International has previously\ndocumented\nhow the DRC’s military authorities, appointed by President Tshisekedi, have used their powers under the state of siege to systematically curtail human rights by harassing journalists and jailing them, and killing human rights defenders and political activists. The authorities have also resorted to the mass, pre-trial detention of those deemed a threat, in facilities where detainees are often held in appalling conditions.\n“All those arbitrarily detained in the provinces of Nord-Kivu and Ituri, including those being held simply for criticizing the state of siege and military authorities, must be immediately released. All members of the DRC’s security forces suspected of committing human rights violations must be brought to justice in fair trials,” said Tigere Chagutah.\n“The DRC authorities should take immediate steps to ensure that prison conditions are meaningfully improved. Inmates must be granted access to nutritious food, clean water, sanitation, and medical care.”\nInstead of further repressing the rights to freedom of expression and peaceful assembly, the DRC authorities should allow people to freely exercise their human rights and find effective measures to address security issues, ahead of the run-up to the general election scheduled for December this year.\n“In consultation with affected communities and civil society groups, the DRC authorities should identify and implement effective measures of protecting civilians and improving security, without further undermining human rights,” said Tigere Chagutah.\nBackground:\nPresident Tshisekedi enforced the “state of siege” in the provinces of Nord-Kivu and Ituri from 6 May 2021. Claiming a radical but necessary step to swiftly stem armed groups and restore security, he drastically restricted human rights and ordered military courts to prosecute civilians. He appointed army and police officers to replace civilian administration including elected Governors and suspended elected provincial assemblies.\nUnder the DRC’s Constitution, a state of siege is defined as an exceptional, temporary measure that may be declared for an initial period of 30 days. If parliament ratifies an extension, it may be extended for subsequent periods of 15 days.\n\nThe International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights, to which the DRC is a party, allows states to derogate from certain human rights provisions, yet the application of such restrictions must meet certain requirements including notifying the UN Secretary-General in writing, explaining the circumstances that justify any derogations. The DRC authorities have not complied with this requirement.\nThe African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights, to which the DRC is also a state party, does not allow for derogation from any of its provisions under any circumstances.\nThe state of siege must also not be used as a pretext to clamp down on human rights.\nThe ever-deteriorating security situation in eastern DRC, including in the provinces of Nord-Kivu and Ituri, has resulted in the deaths of over 4,000 civilians, while more than two million have been forcibly displaced since 2021, according to figures from the UN and the Kivu Security Tracker.\nAfrica\nArmed Conflict\nDemocratic Republic of the Congo\nEast Africa, the Horn and Great Lakes\nFreedom of Movement\nNews\nPress Release\nNews\nRelated Content\nCountry\nDemocratic 2", "exact_excerpt": "Inscription en doctorat le 12/10/2022\nEtablissement(s) :\nUniversité Paris-Panthéon-Assas\nEcole(s) doctorale(s) :\nEGIC Economie, gestion, information et communication\nPartenaire(s) de recherche :\nLaboratoire :\nCentre d'analyse et de recherche interdisciplinaires sur les médias\nMots clés\nFR\n|\nEN\nMots clés libres\nStratégies discursives\nDiscours politique\nRhétorique politique\nRésumé\nFR\n|\nEN\nCette thèse examine les stratégies discursives utilisées par Félix-Antoine Tshisekedi, Président de la République Démocratique du Congo, dans ses discours sur l'état de la Nation de 2018 à 2023. En s'appuyant sur l'analyse critique du discours (Fairclough, Van Dijk), l'étude explore comment ces discours sont délibérément conçus pour légitimer l'autorité du Président, renforcer son pouvoir et mobiliser l'opinion publique dans un contexte politique instable. Trois dimensions sont analysées : les techniques de persuasion, les thèmes récurrents comme la sécurité et l'économie, ainsi que l'adaptation des discours aux changements sociaux et politiques. Cette recherche met en lumière les dynamiques rhétoriques à travers lesquelles le langage politique devient un outil stratégique de gouvernance, influençant les perceptions et les comportements politiques en RDC. Elle offre également une contribution originale à l'étude du discours politique en Afrique, en analysant des discours inédits dans la recherche scientifique.\ntheses.fr\nLe moteur de recherche theses.fr recense l’ensemble des thèses de doctorat soutenues en France depuis 1985, les sujets de thèse préparés au sein des établissements de l’enseignement supérieur français, et les personnes impliquées dans la recherche doctorale française. La base de données est en constante évolution.\nEn savoir plus\nRessources", "excerpt_sha256": "63ac9d7f0a0b0093b988ff53d7cf7e8aa255be27a54bfa0c55f23b573209c3a5", "verification_status": "accepted", "end_unit": 2, "locator": "extracted HTML block 2", "exact_excerpt": "Inscription en doctorat le 12/10/2022\nEtablissement(s) :\nUniversité Paris-Panthéon-Assas\nEcole(s) doctorale(s) :\nEGIC Economie, gestion, information et communication\nPartenaire(s) de recherche :\nLaboratoire :\nCentre d'analyse et de recherche interdisciplinaires sur les médias\nMots clés\nFR\n|\nEN\nMots clés libres\nStratégies discursives\nDiscours politique\nRhétorique politique\nRésumé\nFR\n|\nEN\nCette thèse examine les stratégies discursives utilisées par Félix-Antoine Tshisekedi, Président de la République Démocratique du Congo, dans ses discours sur l'état de la Nation de 2018 à 2023. En s'appuyant sur l'analyse critique du discours (Fairclough, Van Dijk), l'étude explore comment ces discours sont délibérément conçus pour légitimer l'autorité du Président, renforcer son pouvoir et mobiliser l'opinion publique dans un contexte politique instable. Trois dimensions sont analysées : les techniques de persuasion, les thèmes récurrents comme la sécurité et l'économie, ainsi que l'adaptation des discours aux changements sociaux et politiques. Cette recherche met en lumière les dynamiques rhétoriques à travers lesquelles le langage politique devient un outil stratégique de gouvernance, influençant les perceptions et les comportements politiques en RDC. Elle offre également une contribution originale à l'étude du discours politique en Afrique, en analysant des discours inédits dans la recherche scientifique.\ntheses.fr\nLe moteur de recherche theses.fr recense l’ensemble des thèses de doctorat soutenues en France depuis 1985, les sujets de thèse préparés au sein des établissements de l’enseignement supérieur français, et les personnes impliquées dans la recherche doctorale française. The highly anticipated announcement includes Judith Suminwa as Prime Minister, along with 54 ministers. This is the first time that a woman will head the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC).\nAfter Tshisekedi’s coalition won the December 2023 legislative elections, delays in forming a government echoed the numerous negotiations on the appointment of members of the Bureau of the National Assembly. Amid increasing\ntensions\nin the Great Lakes region, what trends can be deduced from this new government? In particular, what skills are Tshisekedi seeking to meet the country's multiple challenges?\nIn general, his second term marks a new attempt to instil a presidential dynamic in which the presidency and the executive branch of government dominate political life. However, the DRC’s constitution provides for a semi-presidential system that combines presidential powers with tight Parliamentary checks. How suitable, then, is an assertive presidency in a fragmented political context, with the persistence of armed\ngroups\nin the east of the country?\nThe new government comprises 55% neophytes, including in key posts such as defence and foreign affairs\nThe absence of political heavyweights is notable, with the exception of Jean-Pierre Bemba, who is Deputy Prime Minister in charge of transport. The government Suminwa heads comprises 55% neophytes, including in key posts such as the interior, defence and foreign affairs. Hailing from the president’s party – the Union pour la démocratie et le progrès social (UDPS) – some of these appointees are occupying ministerial posts for the first time.\nIn so doing, Tshisekedi has broken with the usual practice of allocating ministerial posts since the end of the Second Congo War in 2003. The traditional approach aims to seek a compromise between the components of the coalition that contribute to the president’s election. The compromise was enshrined in the DRC’s 2006 constitution and reinforced by the unusual alliance between the UDPS and Joseph Kabila loyalists following Tshisekedi’s 2018 election.\nThe apparent break with this logic is illustrated by the absence of leaders of the main coalition parties that brought Tshisekedi to power in the 2023 election. Also known as ‘moral authorities’, these figures include Vital Kamerhe, newly elected Speaker of Parliament; Modeste Bahati Lukwebo, former senate president; and Sama Lukonde, former prime minister, whose coalition came third in the legislative elections.\nAlthough the heavyweights are absent from government, most have been elected to Parliament\nAlthough the new prime minister wanted to avoid a government with multiple centres of power, there may be other reasons for this change. This is Tshisekedi’s last term in office, which means the next five years will be characterised by a hidden electoral campaign among those seeking to succeed him. Had Tshisekedi given those political heavyweights an opportunity to shine in government, he could easily have been turned into a lame-duck president during his second term.\nHis decision to appoint UDPS loyalists and technocrats aims to ensure a more fluid government operation by insulating the ministers from political dynamics. He is reinforcing the same logic that was present in the\nprevious\ngovernment led by former prime minister Sama Lukonde – a technocrat with no political base.\nBut although the heavyweights are absent from government, most have been elected to Parliament. This will likely strengthen the National Assembly and Senate’s control over the Executive. Kamerhe – the new Speaker of Parliament – isn’t the only political actor with presidential ambitions. However, there is a risk of parliamentary control being abused for political ends at a time when numerous leading figures are already positioning themselves for 2028.\nBy appointing a government that reflects his party, Tshisekedi is breaking with DRC’s post-conflict consensus\nThe main challenge for all players will be the confrontation between Tshisekedi’s presidentialist leanings and the largely semi-presidential nature of the Congolese constitution, which on paper, gives Parliament a predominant role. This is a key difference between the DRC and its Central African neighbours, whose constitutions establish the predominance of the president.\nWould presidentialist rule be appropriate for the DRC? Some point\nout\nthat Mobutu Sese Seko’s presidential regime helped stabilise the country to an extent, in contrast to the disorder inherent in the two-party system of the DRC’s first constitution in 1963. Others believe geographic, linguistic and economic diversity, along with the country’s vast territory, aren’t suited to a concentration of power in a single party or region. For example, the elected representatives of Maniema Province object to their\nabsence", "excerpt_sha256": "d31150f0e3ee5a76f5f0f06183813a40ba94ab7c0db91ba489212176213d930c", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0013-E004", "source_id": "SRC-f5de8aa767712d49", "url": "https://carnegieendowment.org/research/2023/08/what-a-us-drc-zambia-electric-vehicle-batteries-deal-reveals-about-the-new-us-approach-toward-africa?center=middle-east", "title": "What a U.S.-DRC-Zambia Electric Vehicle Batteries Deal Reveals About the New U.S. Approach Toward Africa", "publisher": "Carnegie Endowment for International Peace", "raw_sha256": "83c42717376f75b405115ea79a1824d20f34b9f5b7ec6f5263548d02dc72eab6", "fact_summary": "The EV-battery MOU was nonbinding, contained no concrete action plan, technological-transfer commitments, or specified U.S. financial commitments, and stated that activities depended on fund availability; the analysis therefore characterized it as primarily a political-signaling and first-step instrument whose implementation was not guaranteed.", "question_ids": ["5B.6", "5B.7", "8B.10", "8B.2", "8B.5", "8B.6"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The assessment was published in August 2023 and evaluates the agreement's status during 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The implementation limitation concerns the trilateral arrangement and DRC government capacity; it does not establish that Tshisekedi personally caused the vagueness or failed to implement it.", "limitations": ["Analyst assessment rather than an audit of Tshisekedi's personal conduct.", "No outcome data are provided."], "start_unit": 4, "end_unit": 5, "locator": "extracted HTML block 4 through extracted HTML block 5", "exact_excerpt": "The U.S.-Africa Leaders Summit as a Deal Platform\nThe public signature of the MOU, along with other trade and investment deals, at the U.S.-Africa summit in December 2022 is part of the U.S. government’s strategy to make its relations with the continent more tangible and aligned with African aims of getting better outputs from these deals in alignment with their governments’ national priorities. The signing happened at a very different time compared to past decades, when the DRC was considered less reputable and battery minerals were not regarded as critical or strategic. In today’s geopolitical environment, in which China has taken the lead in building out critical minerals supply chains in Africa, the United States aims to both catch up with and counter China.\nThis public MOU signing seems to be adapted from China’s playbook, especially from Chinese agreements on financing and building hard infrastructure projects in Africa. For example, in 2018,\nChina signed MOUs\nwith thirty-seven African countries and the African Union at a Belt and Road Initiative summit, indicating that efforts to bolster cooperation through summits go very far.\nMOU\ns\nas a First Step\nMOUs are both soft-law and soft-power tools that open the door for future negotiations and contracts between foreign entities. Most MOUs are\nless than ten pages\n, sometimes only a page or two. (The trilateral MOU in this case is five pages.) Thus, signing a MOU makes sense because it allows for due diligence and sourcing of financing to begin without expending resources in legal and accounting procedures that a full-fledged agreement would require; the transaction costs are lower. MOUs are hence used as first-step instruments for the prenegotiation phase of infrastructure and mining contracts and can serve as a framework and basis for further negotiations.\nThe U.S.-DRC-Zambia MOU is so far a simple expression of the three countries’ interest in working together. The signing does not guarantee its implementation, which depends on many factors that the U.S. government alone cannot entirely control or anticipate. It is a nonbinding agreement that offers flexibility and avoids the rigidity of a legally binding contract. Moreover, it can be undone.\nAt the bilateral level, both the United States and China have signed several MOUs with the DRC. The U.S. government has signed three MOUs on mining projects with the country’s Ministry of Mines and the Ministry of Employment, Labour, and Social Security in the last decade. One of the MOUs China signed was for the major Sicomines\nmining deal project\n, which included two annexes related to infrastructure and mining parts of the deal, in 2007. While not specifying financial commitments, that MOU gave additional details on the negotiations and led to a cooperation agreement—the final stage of a MOU that leads to detailed formal contracts signed by both countries.\nA Political Signaling Tool\nAt this stage, the value offered by the U.S.-DRC-Zambia MOU seems more political than actionable and can be considered a push for the U.S. government to have deliverables to point to after the conclusion of the U.S.-Africa summit.\nWhile past bilateral U.S.-DRC MOUs were signed by dedicated ministries and were therefore more specific and less public, the U.S.-DRC-Zambia MOU remains vague. The document does not mention concrete engagements or an action plan, as bilateral MOUs usually do. It specifies neither technological input and transfers nor U.S. financial commitments, saying that “all activities pursued under this MOU are subject to the availability of funds.” It stipulates that it is not intended to be legally binding and does not constitute an obligation.\nThe MOU is a means to position the United States as a contender not only to China but also to European countries that are courting the DRC and other African countries to secure critical minerals. The broad rhetorical style used at this stage is also a means to signal to the respective constituencies in the United States, the DRC, and Zambia that the three governments are getting important things done.\nA More Inclusive U.S. Approach\nWhat is distinct about the U.S.-DRC-Zambia MOU is that it marks the first time that an existing bilateral agreement between two African countries transformed into a trilateral initiative involving three parties. Unlike the Chinese approach that largely centers around bilateral deals in the infrastructure and mining sectors, this MOU includes three parties and evolved out of a bilateral agreement.\nChina makes greater use of government-to-government and less use of government-private sector MOUs. Chinese official entities also issue MOUs at various levels of the Chinese administration (such as the central or provincial levels), while the United States usually involves both the government and the private sector. Yet, in the trilateral MOU, several questions remain open, including whether U.S. firms—known for their deep risk aversion to Africa—are willing to invest in the initiative and whether funds are available.\nThe DRC’s and Zambia’s Input in the Execution of the MOU\nThe U.S.-DRC-Zambia MOU aims to centralize the production of EV batteries in the DRC and Zambia, despite the current trend of mineral extraction happening in Africa but refinement taking place elsewhere. The follow-up negotiations and execution of the MOU will be greatly informed by the extent to which the two African countries can exercise more agency. The countries’ governments seem more aware of this new geopolitical setting, and the ongoing\nreview and renegotiation\n\nof cobalt and copper mining deals between China and the DRC have revealed how the DRC government could increase its agency and play a bigger role in negotiating better mining deals. However, with presidential elections looming (in December 2023 in the DRC), it will be crucial to avoid political interference and conflicts of interests in the negotiation process that may hamper the deals.\nWhether the DRC and Zambia can increase their technical capacities will determine their strength in the negotiation process and shape how much they can influence the MOU’s execution. An upgrade of technical capabilities can be done by associating the right expertise both from within their respective cabinets and relevant ministries and external expertise to conduct meaningful cost-benefit analyses to determine the value of the minerals and potential revenues as well as the best investment opportunities.\nThe MOU’s execution will also be shaped by how the Congolese and Zambian governments\nnavigate great power rivalry\nbetween the United States and China, and to some extent Europe, while prioritizing their populations’ economic and social interests in the long term.\nConclusion\nMOUs feature prominently in Africa-China engagement and are usually preferred by both sides to formal agreements, which the United States and European countries lean on more often. Whether bilateral or trilateral, MOUs provide a framework that can be built upon. This is the case for both the U.S. and Chinese versions.\nFor the U.S.-DRC-Zambia MOU to be effective, the follow-up policy initiatives and negotiation of the agreement should contain concrete measures, have clear benchmarks, and create an oversight mechanism to assure that the objectives are successfully implemented and move beyond political signaling.\nAcknowledgments\nA team of students (Marymegan Wright, Ivana Ramirez, and Camillo Padulli) led by Prince Osaji from the Yale Foreign Policy Initiative were mentored by the author and provided research assistance for this article.\nAbout the Author\nFolashadé Soulé\nFormer Nonresident Scholar, Africa Program\nFolashadé Soulé was a nonresident scholar in the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace’s Africa Program. block 4", "exact_excerpt": "ISS Today\nBy\nNirvaly Mooloo\nResearch Officer, Central Africa and Lake Chad Basin, ISS\nRemadji Hoinathy\nSenior Researcher, Central Africa and Lake Chad Basin, ISS\nFollowing President Félix Tshisekedi’s contested victory in the Democratic Republic of the Congo’s (DRC) December 2023 elections, democracy hangs in the balance. Amid allegations of irregularities and voter disenfranchisement, Tshisekedi’s re-election underscores deep problems with the country’s democratic fabric.\nThe Independent National Electoral Commission (CENI) declared Tshisekedi the winner with over 70% of the\nvotes\n. He was followed by Moïse Katumbi with 18%, Martin Fayulu with 5% and Denis Mukwege with less than 1%.\nMany believe the president’s landslide victory is implausible. Opposition candidates and citizens\nview\nthe country’s synchronised presidential, local, provincial and national polls as a ‘farce’, and calls have been made for a rerun.\nPre-election predictions conducted by\nGeoPoll\nsuggested that Tshisekedi would win on the basis of limited socio-economic improvements such as free education, and a divided opposition. But the extent of his landslide victory had raised questions about the whole process.\nThe national electoral commission didn’t provide a voter register for review and verification\nThe international community, opposition and civil society groups continuously warned about\ndeficiencies\nin CENI’s pre-election preparations. An observer mission of the Catholic Church and Church of Christ in the Congo documented 5 402 significant irregularities at polling stations, such as malfunctioning voting devices, unopened polling stations and ballot stuffing. Site de la Misson d’Observation Électorale (SYMOCEL), a domestic observer group,\nreported\nthat two-thirds of polling stations opened late, and only 57% complied with procedures.\nMoreover, despite CENI announcing that 44 million people (of the total 100 million) had registered to vote, no voter register was provided for review and verification. CENI had reportedly\ndeclined\noffers from the African Union (AU) and United Nations (UN) to audit the electoral roll. Instead, an auditor\n\nreviewed\nthe figures over five days. CENI also failed to increase transparency during voter registration and rejected the opposition National Episcopal Conference of the Congo’s calls for an independent audit of the voter registry.\nThe 2023 elections exhibit minimal progress from the pivotal 2018 polls, which represented the DRC’s first chance for a democratic transfer of power. Both elections have been tainted by similar allegations.\nIn 2018, both the Catholic Church and SYMOCEL reported fraud and irregularities, including being prevented from observing, missing ballots and missing voting machines. Discrepancies arose between tallies of local results compilation centres and polling stations, resulting in the former refusing to endorse the official count.\nFew of SADC’s recommendations for electoral reform were implemented by CENI or the government\nThe AU, UN and Southern African Development Community (SADC) also noted problems with the 2018 polls, with\nSADC\ncalling on Kinshasa to recount the votes. Despite all these issues, the three regional organisations said overall, the election went well. So it’s probably unsurprising that few of the SADC observer mission’s recommendations for electoral reform were implemented by CENI or the government before December’s polls.\nIn the 2023 elections, the international community has again overlooked serious electoral flaws. The UN\ncalled\nfor a probe only into reports of hate speech and inciting violence, and the AU and SADC haven’t taken a definitive position.\nDuring the 2018 elections, Fayulu emerged as a prominent opposition figure, contesting Tshisekedi’s win. Fayulu\nlabelled\nthe contested poll an ‘electoral coup,’ accusing Tshisekedi of striking a deal with outgoing president Joseph Kabila. This sentiment has also tainted the 2023 polls, with opposition candidates calling for joint protests.\nPersistent allegations of irregularities, fraud and political manipulation in 2018 and 2023 have damaged public trust in the electoral process and eroded the credibility of democratic institutions across the DRC. The country desperately needs independent electoral commissions and judicial institutions.\nLike in 2018, the international community has again overlooked serious electoral problems\nThe problem of\nnon-voters\nwill also have far-reaching implications for democracy. The resurgence of M23 and the hundred or so armed groups in east DRC, notably the Wazalendo coalition, meant that voting didn’t happen in Rutshuru and Masisi territories. This disenfranchisement of hundreds of thousands of citizens also marred the 2018 elections and could fuel civil unrest and instability.\nThe government must remain receptive to dialogue with the political opposition on reforming the electoral process. This will be pivotal to quelling tensions and allowing the opposition to participate in governing the nation. Security in Rutshuru and Masisi must also be bolstered, although current dynamics suggest this will take some time.\nTshisekedi must collaborate with CENI to implement reforms that guarantee integrity, transparency and inclusivity in future elections. Without that, public trust in the electoral process and democratic institutions will remain low, with damaging consequences for the DRC’s stability. SADC, the AU and UN can provide support and expertise in this regard.\nExclusive rights to re-publish ISS Today articles have been given to\nDaily Maverick\nin South Africa and\nPremium Times", "excerpt_sha256": "2a6015557be1af8f8d46671b4c2be59cd9221e4b823b746b530590ff3d1e4ae5", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0013-E009", "source_id": "SRC-2085e4ebd4fd46c1", "url": "https://www.issafrica.org/iss-today/drc-misses-another-opportunity-to-build-a-democracy", "title": "DRC misses another opportunity to build a democracy", "publisher": "Institute for Security Studies", "raw_sha256": "d8685a5db741dded567a529efb0ad00261eb29b4d107c4540f8775d1b21e5ce1", "fact_summary": "CENI announced that 44 million people were registered to vote but did not provide the voter register for review and verification; it reportedly declined AU and UN offers to audit the electoral roll, had the figures reviewed over five days, and rejected calls for an independent audit.", "question_ids": ["4B.1", "4B.10", "4B.2", "4B.4", "4B.6", "7B.6", "7B.7", "8B.10", "8B.5", "8B.8"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "These actions concerned the 2023 electoral process.", "ruler_attribution": "The documented decisions are attributed to CENI; the source does not establish Tshisekedi's personal instruction or knowledge, though they occurred under his administration.", "limitations": ["Institutional attribution is to CENI, not directly to the president.", "The article was published in 2024."], "start_unit": 3, "end_unit": 4, "locator": "extracted HTML block 3 through extracted HTML block 4", "exact_excerpt": "ISS Today\nBy\nNirvaly Mooloo\nResearch Officer, Central Africa and Lake Chad Basin, ISS\nRemadji Hoinathy\nSenior Researcher, Central Africa and Lake Chad Basin, ISS\nFollowing President Félix Tshisekedi’s contested victory in the Democratic Republic of the Congo’s (DRC) December 2023 elections, democracy hangs in the balance. Opposition candidates and citizens\nview\nthe country’s synchronised presidential, local, provincial and national polls as a ‘farce’, and calls have been made for a rerun.\nPre-election predictions conducted by\nGeoPoll\nsuggested that Tshisekedi would win on the basis of limited socio-economic improvements such as free education, and a divided opposition. But the extent of his landslide victory had raised questions about the whole process.\nThe national electoral commission didn’t provide a voter register for review and verification\nThe international community, opposition and civil society groups continuously warned about\ndeficiencies\nin CENI’s pre-election preparations. An observer mission of the Catholic Church and Church of Christ in the Congo documented 5 402 significant irregularities at polling stations, such as malfunctioning voting devices, unopened polling stations and ballot stuffing. Site de la Misson d’Observation Électorale (SYMOCEL), a domestic observer group,\nreported\nthat two-thirds of polling stations opened late, and only 57% complied with procedures.\nMoreover, despite CENI announcing that 44 million people (of the total 100 million) had registered to vote, no voter register was provided for review and verification. CENI had reportedly\ndeclined\noffers from the African Union (AU) and United Nations (UN) to audit the electoral roll. Instead, an auditor\n\nreviewed\nthe figures over five days. CENI also failed to increase transparency during voter registration and rejected the opposition National Episcopal Conference of the Congo’s calls for an independent audit of the voter registry.\nThe 2023 elections exhibit minimal progress from the pivotal 2018 polls, which represented the DRC’s first chance for a democratic transfer of power. Both elections have been tainted by similar allegations.\nIn 2018, both the Catholic Church and SYMOCEL reported fraud and irregularities, including being prevented from observing, missing ballots and missing voting machines. Discrepancies arose between tallies of local results compilation centres and polling stations, resulting in the former refusing to endorse the official count.\nFew of SADC’s recommendations for electoral reform were implemented by CENI or the government\nThe AU, UN and Southern African Development Community (SADC) also noted problems with the 2018 polls, with\nSADC\ncalling on Kinshasa to recount the votes. Despite all these issues, the three regional organisations said overall, the election went well. So it’s probably unsurprising that few of the SADC observer mission’s recommendations for electoral reform were implemented by CENI or the government before December’s polls.\nIn the 2023 elections, the international community has again overlooked serious electoral flaws. The UN\ncalled\nfor a probe only into reports of hate speech and inciting violence, and the AU and SADC haven’t taken a definitive position.\nDuring the 2018 elections, Fayulu emerged as a prominent opposition figure, contesting Tshisekedi’s win. Fayulu\nlabelled\nthe contested poll an ‘electoral coup,’ accusing Tshisekedi of striking a deal with outgoing president Joseph Kabila. This sentiment has also tainted the 2023 polls, with opposition candidates calling for joint protests.\nPersistent allegations of irregularities, fraud and political manipulation in 2018 and 2023 have damaged public trust in the electoral process and eroded the credibility of democratic institutions across the DRC. The country desperately needs independent electoral commissions and judicial institutions.\nLike in 2018, the international community has again overlooked serious electoral problems\nThe problem of\nnon-voters\nwill also have far-reaching implications for democracy. The resurgence of M23 and the hundred or so armed groups in east DRC, notably the Wazalendo coalition, meant that voting didn’t happen in Rutshuru and Masisi territories. This disenfranchisement of hundreds of thousands of citizens also marred the 2018 elections and could fuel civil unrest and instability.\nThe government must remain receptive to dialogue with the political opposition on reforming the electoral process. This will be pivotal to quelling tensions and allowing the opposition to participate in governing the nation. Security in Rutshuru and Masisi must also be bolstered, although current dynamics suggest this will take some time.\nTshisekedi must collaborate with CENI to implement reforms that guarantee integrity, transparency and inclusivity in future elections. Without that, public trust in the electoral process and democratic institutions will remain low, with damaging consequences for the DRC’s stability. SADC, the AU and UN can provide support and expertise in this regard.\nExclusive rights to re-publish ISS Today articles have been given to\nDaily Maverick\nin South Africa and\nPremium Times", "excerpt_sha256": "2a6015557be1af8f8d46671b4c2be59cd9221e4b823b746b530590ff3d1e4ae5", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt says CENI had reportedly declined the AU and UN offers, so the attribution is narrowed accordingly.", "The excerpt says an auditor reviewed the figures over five days; it does not explicitly call this an internal audit in the supplied English passage."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0013-E010", "source_id": "SRC-2085e4ebd4fd46c1", "url": "https://www.issafrica.org/iss-today/drc-misses-another-opportunity-to-build-a-democracy", "title": "DRC misses another opportunity to build a democracy", "publisher": "Institute for Security Studies", "raw_sha256": "d8685a5db741dded567a529efb0ad00261eb29b4d107c4540f8775d1b21e5ce1", "fact_summary": "The article reported that few SADC electoral-reform recommendations from the 2018 elections were implemented by CENI or the government before the December 2023 polls, and that persistent allegations of irregularity, fraud and political manipulation in 2018 and 2023 eroded trust in electoral processes and democratic institutions.", "question_ids": ["4B.10", "4B.4", "4B.6", "7B.10", "7B.2", "7B.6", "8B.10", "8B.8"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The reported lack of reform implementation is assessed before the December 2023 election.", "ruler_attribution": "The source attributes nonimplementation to CENI or the government collectively; it does not isolate Tshisekedi's personal responsibility.", "limitations": ["The source does not list which recommendations were or were not implemented.", "The article is a post-election assessment."], "start_unit": 4, "end_unit": 4, "locator": "extracted HTML block 4", "exact_excerpt": "reviewed\nthe figures over five days. People are tired of paying the price for the hostilities between the armed group M23 and the country’s armed forces and their allies, the Wazalendo.\nSince hostilities resumed in October, more than 450,000 people – twice the population of Geneva – have been forced from their homes in North Kivu, pushing up the total number of displaced people in the country to a record 6.9 million.\nOver the past few months, the situation has cycled between periods of fighting and moments of relative calm. Hundreds of thousands of people have crowded into camps but cannot count on having decent living conditions there. Most of the humanitarian assistance being provided is going to these camps, but it is not enough. Certain particularly remote or isolated rural areas remain out of reach and are receiving no or very little assistance, such as in Masisi and Rutshuru territories.\nIt’s a nightmare for people to be forced from their homes time and again, and logistically extremely complex. Each time people move, the teams of the ICRC and other organizations have to re-assess the obstacles people face to meeting their essential needs and respond accordingly.\nAlthough the ICRC has a well-established dialogue with the various weapon-bearers, the clashes are so numerous and so intense that it leaves little room for humanitarian activities and few opportunities to deliver aid.\nIn these conditions, misinformation and hate speech are spreading dangerously, including on social media. We fear there is a real danger of the violence spiralling out of control. The effect of this messaging has been, directly and indirectly, to further jeopardize civilian’s safety and dignity. The hate is causing psychological and social harm and casting doubt on the principles of humanitarian work, endangering humanitarian activities.\nSadly, we have seen a significant rise in the number of children being recruited from the communities of Masisi, Rutshuru and Nyiragongo. Adolescent boys are particularly at risk of being recruited, and girls are at risk of sexual violence.\nLast week, having obtained the necessary security guarantees, we were, thankfully, once again able to cross the front lines and deliver emergency assistance to the most isolated people, some of whom had not received any assistance since this round of fighting began.\nIn Bwito, in Rutshuru territory, our teams provided food and tarpaulins to more than 22,000 people. And thanks to donations of medicines and medical supplies, more than 150,000 people, including those who are wounded, will be able to receive treatment in the region’s health centres.\nWherever we go, our work is to support communities in need. It was gratifying to see how happy the people were to see us, and to hear them say “If you’ve come all this way, it means we haven’t been forgotten”.\n\nThis was just a first step. Even as people are becoming increasingly desperate, we remain as determined as ever to pursue our dialogue with the parties to the conflict, to ensure that the rules of war are followed. We will work unstintingly alongside the Red Cross Society of the Democratic Republic of the Congo to provide neutral, impartial and independent assistance to those who need it most.\nAbout the ICRC\nThe International Committee of the Red Cross (ICRC) is a neutral, impartial and independent organization with an exclusively humanitarian mandate that stems from the Geneva Conventions of 1949. It helps people around the world affected by armed conflict and other violence, doing everything it can to protect their lives and dignity and to relieve their suffering, often alongside its Red Cross and Red Crescent partners.\nFor more information, please contact:\nFatima Sator, ICRC Geneva, tel: +41 79 848 49 08, email:\nfsator@icrc.org\nHalimatou Amadou, ICRC Geneva, tel: +41 79 868 55 83, email:\nhamadou@icrc.org\nRelated\nArticle\n19-06-2026\nWhere is my family? Both projects are financed by the International Development Association (*IDA).\nThe Foundational Economic Governance Reform operation\n, the second DPO in a series of two, supports the government’s reform program, addressing key governance challenges in public finance, market liberalization, and the management of the forestry and extractive industries—with the overall objective of accelerating green, resilient, and inclusive growth and development. As such, the engagement supports measures that will lead to increased transparency as a prerequisite for enhanced government accountability and capacity in sustainably managing DRC’s vast natural resources, including its forests, which represent a carbon sink of global importance.\n“The approval in June 2022 of the\nfirst Development Policy Operation\nin DRC since 2005 represented a turning point in the relationship between DRC and the World Bank. The approval of this second operation is a recognition of the ambitious reform program implemented by the Congolese authorities despite a highly challenging environment,” said\nAlbert Zeufack, World Bank Country Director for the Democratic Republic of Congo, Burundi, Angola, and Sao Tome and Principe\n. “Moving forward, the World Bank will maintain a strong focus on reform implementation to ensure that the expected results of these two operations are achieved and benefit the country’s 100 million people.”\nBuilding on the adoption of long-awaited structural reforms supported under the first DPO, the second operation supports a deepening and broadening of reforms through the implementation of core treasury and accounting functions; strengthening of public procurement procedures; expansion of the digitalization process within the domestic revenue mobilization chain; implementation of the 2020 Telecom Law; the appointment of competitively selected leaders for the power and water utilities as well as the national road fund; strengthened oversight and transparency within the Mining Fund for Future Generations; approval of procedures for the sale/transfer of state-owned enterprise mining assets; and achievement of key milestones towards the completion of a review of current forest concessions.\nThe Girls’ Learning and Empowerment Project will establish the foundations for an effective secondary school learning environment and promote knowledge, skills, and behavior change needed to make teaching more effective and schools safe, inclusive, and free of sexual exploitation and abuse. The project will also provide sexual reproductive health and menstrual health management education in secondary school classrooms.\n“In the context of the free primary education policy, implemented since 2019, this project offers an opportunity to achieve sustainable results in improving girls' education at the secondary level in DRC. By supporting learning and girls’ empowerment in the country’s provinces with the lowest girls’ participation in secondary education, the project will help provide access to quality secondary education services for the most vulnerable population. These human capital investments will be critical for boosting growth and improving welfare of the country,\" said\nHamoud Abdel Wedoud Kamil, World Bank Lead Education Specialist for the Democratic Republic of Congo.\nThe World Bank was among the first organizations to support the ambitious free primary education policy adopted and implemented by the Congolese government. As a result, more than 3.6 million additional students are enrolled in public primary schools in 2021-22 as compared to 2017-18. The World Bank’s new engagement in secondary education builds on existing large-scale support in the primary education sector and is aligned with the government's objective of strengthening general, technical, and vocational secondary education.\n*The World Bank’s International Development Association (IDA), established in 1960, helps the world’s poorest countries by providing grants and low to zero-interest loans for projects and programs that boost economic growth, reduce poverty, and improve poor people’s lives. IDA is one of the largest sources of assistance for the world’s 74 poorest countries, 39 of which are in Africa. Resources from IDA bring positive change to the 1.3 billion people who live in IDA countries. Since 1960, IDA has provided $496 billion to 114 countries. Annual commitments have increased steadily and averaged about $34.7 billion over the last three years (FY20-FY22), with about 70 percent going to Africa. The approval of this second operation is a recognition of the ambitious reform program implemented by the Congolese authorities despite a highly challenging environment,” said\nAlbert Zeufack, World Bank Country Director for the Democratic Republic of Congo, Burundi, Angola, and Sao Tome and Principe\n. “Moving forward, the World Bank will maintain a strong focus on reform implementation to ensure that the expected results of these two operations are achieved and benefit the country’s 100 million people.”\nBuilding on the adoption of long-awaited structural reforms supported under the first DPO, the second operation supports a deepening and broadening of reforms through the implementation of core treasury and accounting functions; strengthening of public procurement procedures; expansion of the digitalization process within the domestic revenue mobilization chain; implementation of the 2020 Telecom Law; the appointment of competitively selected leaders for the power and water utilities as well as the national road fund; strengthened oversight and transparency within the Mining Fund for Future Generations; approval of procedures for the sale/transfer of state-owned enterprise mining assets; and achievement of key milestones towards the completion of a review of current forest concessions.\nThe Girls’ Learning and Empowerment Project will establish the foundations for an effective secondary school learning environment and promote knowledge, skills, and behavior change needed to make teaching more effective and schools safe, inclusive, and free of sexual exploitation and abuse. The project will also provide sexual reproductive health and menstrual health management education in secondary school classrooms.\n“In the context of the free primary education policy, implemented since 2019, this project offers an opportunity to achieve sustainable results in improving girls' education at the secondary level in DRC. By supporting learning and girls’ empowerment in the country’s provinces with the lowest girls’ participation in secondary education, the project will help provide access to quality secondary education services for the most vulnerable population. These human capital investments will be critical for boosting growth and improving welfare of the country,\" said\nHamoud Abdel Wedoud Kamil, World Bank Lead Education Specialist for the Democratic Republic of Congo.\nThe World Bank was among the first organizations to support the ambitious free primary education policy adopted and implemented by the Congolese government. As a result, more than 3.6 million additional students are enrolled in public primary schools in 2021-22 as compared to 2017-18. The World Bank’s new engagement in secondary education builds on existing large-scale support in the primary education sector and is aligned with the government's objective of strengthening general, technical, and vocational secondary education.\n*The World Bank’s International Development Association (IDA), established in 1960, helps the world’s poorest countries by providing grants and low to zero-interest loans for projects and programs that boost economic growth, reduce poverty, and improve poor people’s lives. IDA is one of the largest sources of assistance for the world’s 74 poorest countries, 39 of which are in Africa. Resources from IDA bring positive change to the 1.3 billion people who live in IDA countries. Since 1960, IDA has provided $496 billion to 114 countries. Annual commitments have increased steadily and averaged about $34.7 billion over the last three years (FY20-FY22), with about 70 percent going to Africa. Learn more online: IDA.worldbank.org. #IDAworks\nContacts\nMedard Lobota\nmlobota@worldbankgroup.org\nKinshasa\nFlore de Préneuf\nfdepreneuf@worldbankgroup.org\nWashington\nDaniella Van Leggelo
